Appendix A
Background and Previous Work

A.1
Background

In the appendix, for some integer
k\geq 0
, we use
[k]
to denote the set
\{1,2,\dots,k\}
(with
[0]=\emptyset
).

Graph preliminaries.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a set of variables in a DAG
\mathcal{G}
over variables
\mathbf{V}
.
We define four kinship relations:

1.

Parents
of
\mathbf{X}
, denoted
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
:
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}=\{Y\in\mathbf{V}\mid Y\to X\text{ for some }X\in\mathbf{X}\}\cup\mathbf{X}
.

2.

Ancestors
of
\mathbf{X}
, denoted
\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
:
\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}=\{Y\in\mathbf{V}\mid\text{ there is a directed path from }Y\text{ to }X\text{ for some }X\in\mathbf{X}\}\cup\mathbf{X}
.

3.

Descendants
of
\mathbf{X}
, denoted
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
:
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}=\{Y\in\mathbf{V}\mid\text{ there is a directed path from }X\text{ to }Y\text{ for some }X\in\mathbf{X}\}\cup\mathbf{X}

4.

Non-descendants
of
\mathbf{X}
, denoted
\mathit{Nd}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
:
\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Note that
\mathit{Nd}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
does not include
\mathbf{X}
.

Readers may be familiar with spouses of a variable
X
as variables
Y
such that
X
and
Y
are both the parent of some
W
. We use a different sense of spouse consistent with
(
Pearl 2000
;
Richardson 2003
)
, defined in Section
2
.

The ordered local Markov property.

We define the ordered local Markov property
(
Richardson 2003
)
for semi-Markovian causal DAGs and its basic components below.

Definition A.1.1
.

(Markov Blanket (MB))
(
Richardson 2003
)

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
X
be a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
and
\mathbf{S}
an ancestral set in
\mathcal{G}
such that
X\in\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
.
Then, the Markov blanket of
X
with respect to the induced subgraph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}
, denoted
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
, is defined as
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}\setminus\{X\}
.

Definition A.1.2
.

(Maximal Ancestral Set (MAS))
(
Richardson 2003
)

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
X
be a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
and
\mathbf{S}
an ancestral set in
\mathcal{G}
such that
X\in\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
.
Then,
\mathbf{S}
is said to be maximal with respect to the Markov blanket
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
if, for any ancestral set
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
such that
X\in\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
and
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we have
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{S}^{\prime}
.

We state Richardson’s
ordered local Markov property
(with quantification MASs instead of all ancestral sets
(
Richardson 2003
, Section 3.1)
).

Definition A.1.3
.

(The Ordered Local Markov Property (LMP,
\prec
))
(
Richardson 2003
)

A probability distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
over variables
\mathbf{V}
is said to satisfy the ordered local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to the consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
if, for any variable
X
and ancestral set
\mathbf{S}
such that
X\in\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
and
\mathbf{S}
is maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
,

X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}\setminus(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\})\mid\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}\text{ in }P(\mathbf{v}).

Finally, we introduce the following collections to understand the web of ancestral sets, MBs, and MASs.

Definition A.1.4
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
, a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, and a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
,
define three collections:

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{X}&=\{X\in\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\mid\ \mathbf{S}\text{ is ancestral }\},\\
\mathcal{Z}_{X}&=\{\mathbf{Z}\mid\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}\text{ for some }\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}\},\text{ and }\\
\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}&=\{\mathbf{S}^{+}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}\mid\mathbf{S}^{+}\text{ is maximal w.r.t. }\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}\}.\end{split}

◼ Example A.1.1
.

Consider
H
in
\mathcal{G}^{2}
(Fig.
1(b)
). We have a c-component
\mathcal{C}(7)_{\mathcal{G}^{2}}=347
. The ancestral set
\mathbf{S}=12347
induces
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({7,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=234
. The MAS with respect to this MB is
\mathbf{S^{+}}=1234567
, resulting in the CI
7\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}156\mid 234
invoked by (LMP,
\prec
).
∎

It is known that GMP and (LMP,
\prec
) are equivalent: for a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, a probability distribution satisfies the global Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
if and only if it satisfies the local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}

(
Richardson 2003
, Thm. 2, Section 3.1)
.

The following lemma provides a (poly-time) test for whether a given set is maximal with respect to the MB that it induces.

Lemma A.1.1
.

(Testing Maximality of Ancestral Set)
(
Richardson 2003
, Lemma. 5)

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
X
be a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
An ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
is maximal with respect to the Markov blanket
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
if and only if:

\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({h(X,\mathbf{S})}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}

where

h(X,\mathbf{S})=\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\}).

A note on Markov blankets.

We offer some clarification on the term ‘Markov blanket’ as used in this paper (Def.
A.1.1
), introduced by
(
Richardson 2003
)
.
The more widely known concept of a Markov blanket is due to
(
Pearl 1988
, Def. 3.12)
.
Given a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
and a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}
, a Pearlian Markov blanket (abbreviated as PMB) is a set of variables
\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathbf{V}\setminus\{X\}
such that
X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{V}\setminus(\{X\}\cup\mathbf{Z})\mid\mathbf{Z}
.
Returning to Fig.
1(b)
, the variable
8
has a PMB
\mathbf{Z}_{1}=2346
since
7\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}15\mid 2346
.

\mathbf{Z}_{1}
is not an MB (per Def.
A.1.1
).

8
has another PMB
\mathbf{Z}_{2}=234
since
7\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}156\mid 234
.

\mathbf{Z}_{2}
is also, coincidentally, an MB, though not all MBs are PMBs.
The key differences between MBs and PMBs are twofold:

1.

In the definition of an MB, we choose an ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
containing
X
, and require that
X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}\setminus(\{X\}\cup\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{})\mid\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
holds; the MB separates
X
from all other variables in
\mathbf{S}
but not necessarily those in
\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}
. The PMB must separate
X
from all other variables in
\mathbf{V}
.

2.

A given ancestral set
\mathbf{S}
induces exactly one MB for a variable
X
. However, there may be multiple PMBs for
X
. An MB is more akin to the notion of a
Markov boundary

(
Pearl 1988
, Def. 3.12)
5
5

5

A Markov boundary is a minimal (Pearlian) Markov blanket, such that any strict subset of the Markov boundary no longer separates the variable from all other variables in the graph.
, in the sense that it is ‘minimal’; removing any variable
Y
from the MB
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
no longer guarantees the independence
X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}\setminus(\{X\}\cup(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{Y\}))\mid\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{Y\}
.

MBs, therefore, are closely related to PMBs but with additional features needed to define and ensure that (LMP,
\prec
) is equivalent to GMP.

A.2
Related Work

In this section, we expand on the Markov properties and algorithms to enumerate them summarised in Table
A.2.1
.

For model testing, a Markov property which invokes only a polynomial number of CI tests is ideal.
However, currently known poly-size properties assume either 1) there is no latent confounding between variables, or 2) the given causal DAG does not contain any directed mixed cycles, or 3) the observational distribution satisfies certain additional constraints
(
Kang and Tian 2009
)
.
Intuitively, a directed mixed cycle is a cycle formed by walking through arrows in one direction.
For instance, in the causal DAG
\mathcal{G}^{2}
(Fig.
1(b)
), the path
2\rightarrow 3\rightarrow 7\leftrightarrow 3
is a directed mixed cycle.
Directed mixed cycles are commonly found in semi-Markovian DAGs – even in the basic bow pattern, in which a variable
X
is a cause of
Y
and
X,Y
have a latent confounder
(
Pearl 2000
)
.
There is no known poly-sized Markov property for the general setting.

There are two known Markov properties for Markovian causal DAGs.

1.

LMP: The local Markov property
(
Pearl 1988
;
Lauritzen et al. 1990
;
Lauritzen 1996
)
.
LMP specifies a linear number of CIs in total: one for each variable
X
, stating that
X
is conditionally independent of its non-descendants given its parents.

2.

PMP: The pairwise Markov property
(
Pearl and Meshkat 1999
)
.
For a graph with
n
variables, PMP invokes
O(n^{2})
CIs: more specifically, one CI for each pair of non-adjacent variables.
PMP assumes that the given probability distribution is a compositional graphoid: that is, it additionally satisfies the intersection and composition axioms.

The intersection and composition axioms do not hold in arbitrary distributions. The intersection axioms holds, for example, in distributions which have full support (
P(\mathbf{v})>0
for all
\mathbf{v}
), e.g., a multivariate Gaussian. Composition holds in multivariate Gaussians and in probability distributions that are faithful to some DAG.

The following are known Markov properties for semi-Markovian causal DAGs.

Coverage

Scalability

Property

Latents

Any Prob. Distr.

Poly-size CIs

Poly-Delay

✗

✓

✓

✓

▲

✓

✓

\prec
)

)

(ours)

✓

✓

Table A.2.1:
Summary of properties and algorithms to enumerate CIs invoked by such properties.
The first column denotes if the property applies to graphs with unobserved confounders; the second, if it applies to arbitrary observational distributions; the third, if it invokes a polynomial number of CIs; the fourth, if there is a poly-delay algorithm to list its invoked CIs.

✓
denotes an addressed area.
✗
denotes an unaddressed area.
▲ denotes that DAGs may contain unobserved variables but not directed mixed cycles
(
Kang and Tian 2009
)
, or the input is a MAG, a tranformation of a DAG
(
Richardson and Spirtes 2002
)
.
▲
denotes that further assumptions must be made on the probability distribution.

1.

RLMP: The reduced local Markov property
(
Kang and Tian 2009
)
.
RLMP invokes a linear number of CIs in total, one for each variable.
RLMP states that a variable is independent of the variables that are neither its descendants nor the descendants of its spouses, conditioning on its parents.
The property assumes that the given probability distribution satisfies the composition axiom and the DAG has no directed mixed cycles.

2.

(RLMP,
\prec
): The ordered reduced local Markov property
(
Kang and Tian 2009
)
.
Given a specific ordering of variables called a
c-ordering

(
Kang and Tian 2009
)
, (RLMP,
\prec
) invokes a linear number of CIs in total.
(RLMP,
\prec
) states that each variable is independent of its predecessors (excluding its spouses) in a c-ordering, given its parents.
The property assumes that the given probability distribution satisfies the composition axiom and the DAG has no directed mixed cycles.

3.

PMP-C: The pairwise Markov property
(
Kang and Tian 2009
)
.
Given a c-ordering, PMP-C invokes
O(n^{2})
many CIs:
more specifically, one CI for each pair of non-adjacent variables.
PMP-C assumes that the given probability distribution satisfies the composition axiom and the DAG has no directed mixed cycles.

4.

PMP-RS: The pairwise Markov property given by
(
Richardson and Spirtes 2002
)
. PMP-RS invokes
O(n^{2})
many CIs, one for each pair of non-adjacent variables, for a given
maximal ancestral graph
(MAG). A semi-Markovian DAG can be transformed into a MAG which encodes exactly the same CIs. It thus suffices to test CIs in the resultant MAG
(
Shipley and Douma 2021
)
. However, the equivalence between this pairwise Markov property and the global Markov property has only been proved for probability distributions that are compositional graphoids
(
Lauritzen and Sadeghi 2018
)
.

5.

S-Markov: The
S
-Markov property
(
Kang and Tian 2009
)
.
S-Markov relaxes the assumption of the given graph containing no directed mixed cycles.
Still, S-Markov assumes that the observational distribution satisfies the composition axiom.
For each variables in the graph that can be c-ordered, S-Markov invokes a linear number of CIs.
However, for variables that are not c-ordered, S-Markov relies on the ordered local Markov property (LMP,
\prec
), which, as discussed, is exponential-sized.

CIs are the only type of constraint that Markovian DAGs impose on the observational distribution. In the non-Markovian case, however, DAGs may encode more complex equality and inequality constraints such as
Verma constraints

(
Verma and Pearl 1990
)
.
While such constraints are outside the scope of this work, there are algorithms that list these constraints in addition to CIs.
However, these algorithms do not run in poly-delay.

Appendix B
C-LMP and the Ordered Local Markov Property

(LMP,
\prec
) is a well-known Markov property that applies to arbitrary observational distributions and causal graphs with unobserved confounders. In this section, we first explain how naively following the definition of (LMP,
\prec
) can take exponential time to output just one CI.
Next, we characterize (LMP,
\prec
) in more depth and show how ACs (Def.
4
) can be used to compute the CIs that (LMP,
\prec
) invokes.

B.1
Brute-Force Listing of CIs Invoked by (LMP,
\prec
)

Algorithm B.1.1

ListCIBF
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
)

1:

Input:

\mathcal{G}
a causal diagram;
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
an ordering consistent with
\mathcal{G}
.

2:

Output:
Listing CIs invoked by (LMP,
\prec
) for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.

3:

for
each
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}

do

4:

for
each ancestral set
\mathbf{S}
such that
X\in\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}

do

5:

if

\mathbf{S}
is maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
6
6

6

A poly-time test for whether an ancestral set
\mathbf{S}
is maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
is shown in Lemma
A.1.1
.

then

6:

Output
X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}\setminus(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\})|\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}

(a)
\mathcal{G}^{e1}

(b)
\mathcal{G}^{e2}

Figure B.1.1:

Examples showing that a brute-force approach (
ListCIBF
) may take exponential time to output one CI invoked by (LMP,
\prec
).

By definition, we can list the CIs invoked by (LMP,
\prec
) (Def.
A.1.3
) by enumerating over MASs.
However, it is unclear how to enumerate over MASs.
Each MAS is defined relative to an MB, and each MB is defined relative to an ancestral set.
Then, an immediate approach is to iterate over all ancestral sets
\mathbf{S}
, verifying if
\mathbf{S}
is maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
before we output its corresponding CI constraint.
We implement this approach in the algorithm
ListCIBF
(Alg.
B.1.1
).

◼ Example B.1.1
.

Consider the DAG
\mathcal{G}^{e1}
(Fig.
1(a)
) with consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}\}
.

ListCIBF
(
\mathcal{G}^{e1},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
) outputs five CIs invoked by (LMP,
\prec
):
A_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{A_{1}\}
,
A_{3}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
,
B_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}
,
B_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},B_{2}\}
, and
B_{3}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},B_{2}\}\mid\{A_{3}\}
.
∎

In Ex.
B.1.1
, given
X=B_{3}
,
ListCIBF
(
\mathcal{G}^{e1},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
) iterates over
2^{4}
different ancestral sets
\mathbf{S}
with
B_{3}\in\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}
, all of which produce the same
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({B_{3},\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\{A_{3}\}
.
However, only
\mathbf{S}^{+}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}
is maximal with respect to this MB, resulting in the CI:
B_{3}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},B_{2}\}\mid\{A_{3}\}
.

ListCIBF
goes over
2^{4}
different ancestral sets to output this CI.
Next, we generalize this example to show that
ListCIBF
may iterate over exponentially many ancestral sets (with respect to the number of variables in
\mathcal{G}
) that produce the same MB.

◼ Example B.1.2
.

In
\mathcal{G}^{e2}
(Fig.
1(b)
) with
2n
nodes, there are
2^{n-1}+2^{n-2}-1
ancestral sets and
n
of them are maximal.
∎

In other words, iterating over all ancestral sets naively is potentially sub-optimal.

In the following lemma, we make a key observation: while there may be many ancestral sets producing the same MB (so that
|\mathcal{S}_{X}|>|\mathcal{Z}_{X}|
), exactly one ancestral set is maximal with respect to this MB.
As a result,
ListCIBF
may take exponential time to output just one new CI.

Lemma B.1.1

(One-to-one Correspondence between
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
and
\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
X
be a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
There is a bijection
f:\mathcal{Z}_{X}\to\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
given by
f(\mathbf{Z})=\mathbf{S}^{+}
where
\mathbf{S}^{+}\in\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
is an ancestral set maximal with respect to
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
. The inverse of
f
,
g:\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}\to\mathcal{Z}_{X}
, is given by
g(\mathbf{S}^{+})=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}
.

◼ Example B.1.3
.

Continuing Ex.
B.1.1
.
Given a variable
B_{3}
, there exists only one MAS
\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}
with respect to the MB
\mathbf{Z}=\{A_{3}\}
of
B_{3}
.
We have
\mathcal{Z}_{B_{3}}=\{\{A_{3}\}\}
and
\mathcal{S^{+}}_{B_{3}}=\{\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}\}
.

\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}
maps uniquely to
\{A_{3}\}
, and vice versa.
∎

B.2
Computing MBs and MASs using ACs

Listing CIs invoked by (LMP,
\prec
) is challenging due to the many-to-one mapping from ancestral sets to CIs. Minimally, we want to be able to list these CIs without brute-force iteration. Fundamental to our solution is the fact that multiple ancestral sets induce the same CI only because they induce the same AC (Def.
4
).

Observe that exponentially many ancestral sets may induce the same AC. For instance, in
\mathcal{G}^{e1}
(Fig.
1(a)
) with
X=B_{3}
,
2^{4}
different ancestral sets
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{B_{3}}
induce the same AC,
\mathcal{C}(B_{3})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}=\{A_{3},B_{3}\}
.

We show that all ancestral sets inducing the same MB and MAS must induce the same AC.

Proposition B.2.1

(Equality of MBs Implies Equality of ACs)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, for any variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
and any ancestral sets
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
, if
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
.

Moreover, the converse is also true: all ancestral sets inducing the same AC must induce the same MB and MAS. In particular, for a variable
X
, given
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}
for some ancestral set
\mathbf{S}
, we can compute
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
and the MAS
\mathbf{S^{+}}
relative to
\mathbf{Z}
in poly-time without using
\mathbf{S}
.
The following results show how MB and MAS can be computed from AC.

Proposition B.2.2

(Construction of MB from AC)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
X
be a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
Fix an ancestral c-component
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
.
For any ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}=\mathbf{C}
, we have
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
.

Proposition B.2.3

(Construction of MAS from AC)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
X
be a variable
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
Fix an ancestral c-component
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
.
For any ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}=\mathbf{C}
, the unique ancestral set
\mathbf{S}^{+}\in\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
maximal with respect to the Markov blanket
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
is given by
\mathbf{S}^{+}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
.

◼ Example B.2.1
.

Consider the DAG
\mathcal{G}^{e1}
(Fig.
1(a)
) with consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}\}
.
Given a variable
B_{3}
,
\mathbf{C}=\{A_{3},B_{3}\}
is an AC relative to
B_{3}
.
We compute the MB
\mathbf{Z}
from
\mathbf{C}
as follows:
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\{A_{3},B_{3}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{B_{3}\}=\{A_{3}\}=\mathbf{Z}
.
For all ancestral sets
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{B_{3}}
, we have
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({B_{3},\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(B_{3})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}\setminus\{B_{3}\}=\{A_{3}\}=\mathbf{Z}
.
The MAS
\mathbf{S^{+}}
relative to
\mathbf{Z}
is given by
\mathbf{S^{+}}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\{A_{3},B_{3}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\{A_{3},B_{3}\}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\emptyset}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq B_{3}}=\mathbf{S}^{+}
.
∎

These results, in part, motivate our definition of a local Markov property via ancestral c-components i.e., C-LMP (Def.
5
). In fact, we can show the following equivalence between C-LMP and (LMP,
\prec
).

Theorem B.2.1

(Correspondence between C-LMP and (LMP,
\prec
))
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph and
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
a consistent ordering.
The c-component local Markov property and the ordered local Markov property
(
Richardson 2003
)
for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
induce an identical set of conditional independence relations implied by
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathbf{V}
.

Proof.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
\mathcal{L}^{R}
denote the set of CIs implied by the ordered local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, and
\mathcal{L}^{C}
the set of CIs implied by the c-component local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
. We show that
\mathcal{L}^{R}=\mathcal{L}^{C}
.

1.

(
\mathcal{L}^{R}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{C}
) Consider a CI statement in
\mathcal{L}^{R}
of the form

X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}^{+}\setminus(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\})\mid\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}

for some variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
and an ancestral set
\mathbf{S}^{+}\in\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}
. We show that the same CI statement is also in
\mathcal{L}^{C}
.

Let
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}^{+}}}
.
Since
\mathbf{S}^{+}
is ancestral,
\mathbf{C}
is an AC relative to
X
.
By Def.
5
, the following CI is in
\mathcal{L}^{C}
.

X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}^{+^{\prime}}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\mid(\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\})

where

\mathbf{S}^{+^{\prime}}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}

By Prop.
B.2.2
,
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
.
By Prop.
B.2.3
,
\mathbf{S}^{+}=\mathbf{S}^{+^{\prime}}
.
Therefore, the two CI statements are identical, and the given CI from
\mathcal{L}^{R}
is also in
\mathcal{L}^{C}
.

2.

(
\mathcal{L}^{C}\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{R}
) Consider a CI statement in
\mathcal{L}^{C}
of the form

X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}^{+}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}

where

\mathbf{S}^{+}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}

for some variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
and AC
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
. By Def.
4
, there exists an ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}
.
By Prop.
B.2.2
,
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
.
By Prop.
B.2.3
,
\mathbf{S}^{+}
is the unique ancestral set maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
.
By Def.
A.1.3
, the following CI is in
\mathcal{L}^{R}

X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{S}^{+}\setminus(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\})\mid\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}

Therefore, the two CI statements are identical, and the given CI from
\mathcal{L}^{C}
is also in
\mathcal{L}^{R}
.

∎

The equivalence between C-LMP and GMP (Thm.
1
) can also be proved as a corollary of the above Thm.
B.2.1
and the equivalence between (LMP,
\prec
) and GMP
(
Richardson 2003
, Thm. 2, Section 3.1)
.

B.3
Uniqueness Property of ACs

Recall that in (LMP,
\prec
), multiple ancestral sets can induce the same MB. Here, we show this can be remedied using ACs: each MB can be computed from exactly one AC.

Lemma B.3.1

(One-to-one Correspondence between
\mathcal{AC}_{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph,
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
a consistent ordering, and
X
a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
Then, there is a bijection
f:\mathcal{AC}_{X}\to\mathcal{Z}_{X}
given by
f(\mathbf{C})=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
with
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
. The inverse of
f
,
g:\mathcal{Z}_{X}\to\mathcal{AC}_{X}
, is given by
g(\mathbf{Z})=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}
where
\mathbf{S}
is an arbitrary ancestral set in
\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
.

Both Lemma
B.1.1
and Lemma
B.3.1
imply a one-to-one correspondence between ACs and MASs.

Corollary B.3.1

(One-to-one Correspondence between
\mathcal{AC}_{X}
and
\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph,
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
a consistent ordering, and
X
a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
There is a bijection
f:\mathcal{AC}_{X}\to\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
.

Fig.
B.4.1
provides an overview of the relationships among the sets of ancestral sets, ACs, MBs, and MASs.
A core implication is that each CI invoked by (LMP,
\prec
) can be derived from exactly one AC, which we exploit in C-LMP (Def.
5
).

B.4
Proofs

We present proofs of the results in Sections
B.2
and
B.3
. We first prove some technical propositions.

Proposition B.4.1

(AC in Union of Subgraphs)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
over a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
, for any subsets
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{V}
and a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}
, if
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}=\mathbf{C}
then
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}}=\mathbf{C}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{S}_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}
, we have
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
.
To show the other direction, for any variable
U\in\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}}\setminus\{X\}
, let
\pi=\{X\leftrightarrow V_{1},V_{1}\leftrightarrow V_{2},\dots,V_{k-1}\leftrightarrow V_{k},V_{k}\leftrightarrow V_{k+1}=U\}
be the bidirected path from
X
to
U
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}
(for some
k\geq 0
). For each
i\in[k+1]
, we have
V_{i}\in\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}
. We prove by induction on the index
i\in[k+1]
that
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
for each
i\in[k+1]
.

Base case.
If
V_{1}\in\mathbf{S}_{1}
then
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}
implies
V_{1}\in C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=\mathbf{C}
.
Otherwise,
V_{1}\in\mathbf{S}_{2}
implies
V_{1}\in C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}=\mathbf{C}
.

Inductive hypothesis.
If
k\geq 1
, assume for some
i\in[k]
we have
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}=C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
.

Inductive step.
Then, either
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{S}_{1}
or
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{S}_{2}
. If
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{S}_{1}
, then
V_{i}\in C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}
(by the induction hypothesis) and
V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}
implies
V_{i+1}\in C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}
. Otherwise,
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
V_{i}\in C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
(by the induction hypothesis)
V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}
implies
V_{i+1}\in C(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
. By induction, it follows that
U=V_{k+1}\in\mathbf{C}
.
∎

Proof of Prop.
B.2.1
.

Consider
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
. If
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{1}
since
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{1},\ \mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
. This implies
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}\subseteq\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}\subseteq\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{1}
. However,
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}\implies\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
and similarly,
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{S}}}
.
∎

Proposition B.4.2

(MB in Union of Subgraphs)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, for any variable
X\in\mathbf{V}
and any ancestral sets
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
, if
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{Z}
, then
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{Z}
.

Proof.

Consider
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
. If
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then by Prop.
B.2.1
, we have
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
. Since
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}
are ancestral, we have
\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\cup\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}
, hence
\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}
is also ancestral. We get

\displaystyle\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}}\setminus\{X\}

(5)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}}\setminus\{X\}

(By Prop.
B.4.1
since
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}}}\setminus\{X\}

(
\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}
ancestral)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}\setminus\{X\}

(
\mathbf{S}_{1}
ancestral)

\displaystyle=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}

(6)

∎

Figure B.4.1:

An overview of the relationships among ancestral sets, ACs, MBs, MASs.
Exponentially many ancestral sets may map to one AC, MB, or MAS.
On the other hand, there is a one-to-one-correspondence among ACs, MBs and MASs.

We now move to our main results in Sections
B.2
. and
B.3
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1.1
.

First, we show the mapping
f
is well-defined. Given
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
, there exists an ancestral set
\mathbf{S}^{+}\in\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
maximal with respect to
\mathbf{Z}
. It remains to show that there is exactly one such
\mathbf{S}^{+}
. Let
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}\in\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
be ancestral sets maximal with respect to
\mathbf{Z}
. The equality
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{Z}
implies
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{Z}
(by Prop.
B.4.2
). Therefore,
\mathbf{S}_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}
and the maximality of
\mathbf{S}_{1}
implies
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\mathbf{S}_{1}\cup\mathbf{S}_{2}
and
\mathbf{S}_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{1}
. Similarly,
\mathbf{S}_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{S}_{1}=\mathbf{S}_{2}
.

Finally,
g
is well-defined.
f(g(\mathbf{S}^{+}))=f(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{})=\mathbf{S}^{+}
, and
g(f(\mathbf{Z}))=\mathbf{Z}
since
f(\mathbf{Z})=\mathbf{S}^{+}
is maximal with respect to
\mathbf{Z}
if and only if
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{Z}
. Since
f
has a two-sided inverse
g
,
f
is bijective.
∎

Proof of Prop.
B.2.2
.

For any ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
with
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}=\mathbf{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}\backslash\{X\}

(7)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}\backslash\{X\}

(By definition of
\mathbf{S}
)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\backslash\{X\}

(
\mathbf{S}
is ancestral and
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{S}
)

∎

Proof of Prop.
B.2.3
.

For any ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
with
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}=\mathbf{C}
, let
\mathbf{S}^{+}\in\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
be an ancestral set maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Note that
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}
by definition. By Prop.
B.2.1
, we have
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}^{+}}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}=\mathbf{C}
. Then,
\mathbf{S}^{+}
is maximal with respect to
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}
if and only if

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{+}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}^{+}}}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\})}\right)\mathclose{}

(Lemma
A.1.1
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}^{+}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\{X\})}\right)\mathclose{}

(8)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus((\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\})\cup\{X\})}\right)\mathclose{}

(Prop.
B.2.2
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}.

(9)

The uniqueness of
\mathbf{S}^{+}
follows from Lemma
B.1.1
. Note that
\mathbf{S}^{+}
depends only on
\mathbf{C}
, not the particular
\mathbf{S}
such that
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}=\mathbf{C}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.3.1
.

First, we show the mapping
f
is well-defined. Given
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
, by definition, there exists an ancestral set
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}
. Then,
f(\mathbf{C})=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
by Prop.
B.2.2
, so
f(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
holds.

Next, we show that
f
is bijective by exhibiting an inverse
g:\mathcal{Z}_{X}\to\mathcal{AC}_{X}
. Given
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
, fix any
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
(we know such
\mathbf{S}
exists by the definition of
\mathbf{Z}
) and let
g(\mathbf{Z})=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}
.

To see that
g
is well-defined, first note that
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
since
\mathbf{S}
is an ancestral set by assumption. Second, we need to show that
g(\mathbf{Z})
is independent of the particular choice of
\mathbf{S}
(since multiple ancestral sets can induce the same MB). Consider
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Let
\mathbf{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}
and
\mathbf{C}_{2}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
.

We show that
\mathbf{C}_{1}=\mathbf{C}_{2}
.
By Prop.
B.2.2
,
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
and
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
.
From the equality
\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we have
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
and hence
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}
.
Since
\mathbf{S}_{1},\mathbf{S}_{2}
are ancestral, we have
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{1}
, and
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
.
With
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
, we have
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}=\mathbf{C}_{1}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}_{2}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathbf{S}_{2}
,
implying
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}
. By a symmetric argument, we get
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{2}}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}_{1}}}
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{C}_{1}=\mathbf{C}_{2}
.

Finally, we show that
g
is a two-sided inverse of
f
. Given
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
, fix some
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}
. Then

\displaystyle g(f(\mathbf{C}))

\displaystyle=g(\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\})

(10)

\displaystyle=g(\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{})

(Prop.
B.2.2
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}

(By definition of
g
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}

(11)

Given
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
, fix some
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Then,

\displaystyle f(g(\mathbf{Z}))

\displaystyle=f(\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}})

(12)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}

(13)

\displaystyle=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}\setminus\{X\}

(
\mathbf{S}
is ancestral and
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{S}}}\subseteq\mathbf{S}
)

\displaystyle=\mathit{mb}\mathopen{}\left({X,\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}

(14)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Z}

(15)

∎

Proof of Cor.
B.3.1
.

The result follows from Lemma
B.1.1
and Lemma
B.3.1
, composing the bijective mappings
f_{1}:\mathcal{AC}_{X}\to\mathcal{Z}_{X}
and
f_{2}:\mathcal{Z}_{X}\to\mathcal{S^{+}}_{X}
.
∎

Figure B.4.2:
A causal graph
\mathcal{G}
with consistent ordering
Z\prec Y\prec X\prec V_{1}\prec\dots\prec V_{k}
, inducing
\Omega(2^{n})
number of CIs invoked by C-LMP.

Appendix C
Proofs

C.1
Section
3
Proofs

Proposition C.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph and
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
a consistent ordering. For any variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
and any ancestral c-component
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
relative to
X
,

X\perp_{d}\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}.

Proof.

Since
\perp_{d}
satisfies the composition and decomposition axioms, it suffices to show that
X\perp_{d}\{Y\}\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
for every
Y\in\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})
.

Take a variable
Y\in\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})
and some path
\pi=(X,V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{n},Y)
between
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
for
n\geq 1
(note that
X,Y
are non-adjacent by assumption).
Let
\pi^{\prime}=(X,V_{1},\dots,V_{k})
(with
k\leq n
) denote the longest sub-path of
\pi
starting from
X
, not including
Y
, that contains only bidirected edges.
If
\pi^{\prime}=\emptyset
, then
V_{1}\prec X\implies V_{1}\in\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\{X\}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
, hence
\pi
is blocked.
Otherwise, if for some
i\in[k]
,
V_{i}\not\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then
V_{i}
blocks
\pi
. If every
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
, since
\mathbf{C}
is an AC, the existence of
\pi^{\prime}
implies
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
. Then, consider the subpath of
\pi
from
V_{k}
to
Y
. Note that
V_{k},Y
are non-adjacent since
Y\not\in\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
. The sub-path has either
V_{k}\leftarrow V_{k+1}\circ-\circ
or
V_{k}\rightarrow V_{k+1}\rightarrow
, both of which are blocked by
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
. Therefore,
\pi
is blocked, and
X\perp_{d}\{Y\}\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
.
∎

Theorem
1

(Equivalence of C-LMP and GMP)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph and
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
a consistent ordering.
A probability distribution over
\mathbf{V}
satisfies the global Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
if and only if it satisfies the c-component local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
(
Lauritzen et al. 1990
, Prop. 4)
and
(
Richardson 2003
, Thm. 2)
, which is based on the former.

(
\implies
) Prop.
C.1.1
shows that the CIs invoked by C-LMP are a subset of those invoked by GMP. Therefore, if a probability distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
satisfies the GMP for a given DAG
\mathcal{G}
, it necessarily satisfies the C-LMP for
\mathcal{G}
(with respect to any given ordering).

(
\impliedby
) Next, we show that if a probability distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
satisfies the C-LMP for a given DAG
\mathcal{G}
with respect to a given ordering, it necessarily satisfies the GMP for
\mathcal{G}
.
We show the other direction by induction on the number of nodes. Let
I_{k}
be the statement that for a graph
\mathcal{G}
on
k
nodes, if a distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
satisfies the C-LMP for
\mathcal{G}
, then it satisfies the GMP for
\mathcal{G}
. The base case is trivial. Assume for some
k
that
I_{j}
is true for all
j\leq k
. We will show this implies
I_{k+1}
. Fix a graph
\mathcal{G}
with
k+1
nodes, a consistent ordering
\prec
, and a distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
which satisfies the C-LMP for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\prec
. Consider a
d
-separation
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}
for disjoint sets
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
. We need to show that
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
P(\mathbf{v})
.

We claim we can assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{V}
.
First, we show how we can assume
\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{V}
. Consider
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}
, and let
\prec^{\prime}
be the ordering
\prec
but removing variables in
\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, so that
P(\mathbf{a})=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\setminus\mathbf{a}}P(\mathbf{v})
. Since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a subgraph on an ancestral set,
any AC in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is an AC in
\mathcal{G}
, it is easy to see that if
P(\mathbf{v})
satisfies the C-LMP for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\prec
, then
P(\mathbf{a})
satisfies the C-LMP for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\prec^{\prime}
. Since
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
contains no more edges than
\mathcal{G}
, we also have
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. By the inductive assumption for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
P(\mathbf{a})
, which implies
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
P(\mathbf{v})
. Finally, we can extend
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
so that
\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}
(and reduce to the original separation statement using the decomposition axiom). For any
V\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}
, either
V\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
or
V\perp_{d}\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Z}
. Towards contradiction, assume
V
has an active path
\pi_{x}
to some node in
\mathbf{X}
and an active path
\pi_{y}
to some node in
\mathbf{Y}
when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. Then, adjoining
\pi=\pi_{x}\cup\pi_{y}
gives an active path between
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
unless
V
is an inactive collider on this path. However, if
V\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
, the path
V\rightsquigarrow X
to the descendant node
X\in\mathbf{X}
, adjoined with
\pi_{y}
, gives an active path between
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
unless we condition on some descendant of
V
; the same applies if
V\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Y}}\right)\mathclose{}
; and if
V\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
, clearly,
V
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. We thus arrive at a contradiction.

Now, consider a separation
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{V}
. We need to show that
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
P(\mathbf{v})
. Let
V^{*}
be the final node in the ordering
\prec
so that
\mathbf{V}^{\leq V^{*}}=\mathbf{V}
. Since
V\in\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}
, there are three cases to consider:

1.

V^{*}\in\mathbf{X}
.

Since
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}
, we have
\mathbf{X}\setminus\{V^{*}\}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}\setminus\{V^{*}\}}
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}\setminus\{V^{*}\}}
is ancestral, we apply a similar argument as in justifying the assumption that
\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{V}
to get, by the inductive assumption for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, that

\mathbf{X}\setminus\{V^{*}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}\text{ in }P(\mathbf{v}).

Let
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(V^{*})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
. By C-LMP, we have
V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{V}\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{V^{*}\}
.

First, note that
\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
. Towards contradiction, assume that for some
Y\in\mathbf{Y}
, there is a path
\pi:Y\circ\rightarrow V_{1}\leftrightarrow V_{2}\leftrightarrow\dots V_{k}\leftrightarrow V_{k+1}=V^{*}
such that each
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
. By induction on
i\in[k+1]
, we show that
\pi
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
.
For the base case, clearly, the subpath
Y\circ\to V_{1}
of
\pi
is active when conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}
.
Assume that, for some
i\in[k+1]
, the sub-path of
\pi
from
Y
to
V_{i}
is active.
Consider the inductive step. If
V_{i}=V^{*}
, we are done. Otherwise, if
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then
V_{i}
is active in
\pi
when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. If
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then from the inductive assumption, there is a path from
Y
to
V_{i}
plus a path
V_{i}\rightsquigarrow X^{\prime}
to some
X^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}
which is only blocked if
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{i}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{Z}\neq\emptyset
. This again implies that
V_{1}
is active in
\pi
when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. In either case, the subpath of
\pi
from
Y
to
V_{i+1}
is active. This contradicts
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}
. Therefore, we can conclude
\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
.

Second, note that
\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
. This is because
\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
. For any
U\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{},U\in\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z}\implies U\in\mathbf{C}
by definition of
\mathbf{C}
. Therefore,
U\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\implies U\in\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z}
. However, for such
U
, there is a path
\pi:U\leftrightarrow V_{1}\leftrightarrow V_{2}\leftrightarrow\dots V_{k}\leftrightarrow V_{k+1}=V^{*}
with each
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
. By a similar induction as for the claim
\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
, we can show that
\pi
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
, which contradicts
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
.

We return to the CI statement

V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{V}\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{V^{*}\}.

Since
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}
by assumption and
\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
, we can simplify this statement to

\displaystyle V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}\cup((\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z})\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}))

\displaystyle\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{V^{*}\}.

For any variable
W\in\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z}
,
W\in\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
implies there is some variable
B\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{W\}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
, hence
B\in\mathbf{C}
. Therefore,
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
. This further implies

V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\cup((\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Z})\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{V^{*}\}

By the weak union axiom, we get

V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid(\mathbf{X}\setminus\{V^{*}\})\cup\mathbf{Z}

Applying the contraction axiom to
\mathbf{X}\setminus\{V^{*}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
and
V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid(\mathbf{X}\setminus\{V^{*}\})\cup\mathbf{Z}
gives
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
P(\mathbf{v})
.

2.

V^{*}\in\mathbf{Y}
. This is similar to the case
V\in\mathbf{X}
(switching
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
in the proof).

3.

V^{*}\in\mathbf{Z}
. Since
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{G}
, we have
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}\setminus\{V^{*}\}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}\setminus\{V^{*}\}}
.
Since
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}\setminus\{V^{*}\}}
is a subgraph on an ancestral set,
we apply a similar argument as in justifying the assumption that
\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{V}
to get, by the inductive assumption for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, that

\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}\setminus\{V^{*}\}\text{ in }P(\mathbf{v}).

Let
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(V^{*})_{\mathcal{G}}
. By C-LMP, we have
V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{V}\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{V^{*}\}
.

First, we show that either
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{Y}=\emptyset
or
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{X}=\emptyset
. Assume, toward contradiction, that
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{Y}\neq\emptyset
and
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{X}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, there are variables
Y\in\mathbf{Y},X\in\mathbf{X}
and a path
\pi:Y\circ\rightarrow V_{1}\leftrightarrow V_{2}\leftrightarrow\dots V_{k}\leftarrow\circ V_{k+1}=X
for some
k\geq 0
. Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a subpath of
\pi
such that one endpoint node of
\pi^{\prime}
is in
\mathbf{X}
, the other endpoint node in
\mathbf{Y}
, and all intermediate nodes (if any) are in
\mathbf{Z}
. It is easy to see
\pi^{\prime}
must exist since
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
if for each
i\in[k]
, we have
V_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}
; otherwise, we can construct
\pi^{\prime}
by removing variables from
\pi
. Then,
\pi^{\prime}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
, which contradicts
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
.

Moreover,
\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
because
\mathbf{C}
is defined over
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{Z}
. Thus,
\mathbf{X}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
.

Return to the CI statement:
V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{V}\setminus(\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{V^{*}\}
.
If
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{Y}=\emptyset
, this simplifies to
V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{X}\cup(\mathbf{Z}\setminus\{V^{*}\})
by an argument similar to Case (1). The contraction axiom applied to
V^{*}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{X}\cup(\mathbf{Z}\setminus\{V^{*}\})
and
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}\setminus\{V^{*}\}
gives
\mathbf{X}\cup\{V^{*}\}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}\setminus\{V^{*}\}
. Applying the weak union axiom to this last CI, we get
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
. A similar argument applies if
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{X}=\emptyset
.

∎

Corollary
1

(Equivalence of C-LMP and the Ordered Local Markov Property)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph and
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
a consistent ordering.
A probability distribution over
\mathbf{V}
satisfies the ordered local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
if and only if it satisfies the c-component local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.

Proof.

By Thm.
1
, a probability distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
over
\mathbf{V}
satisfies the C-LMP for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
if and only if it satisfies the GMP for
\mathcal{G}
. By
(
Richardson 2003
, Thm. 2, Section 3.1)
, a probability distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
over
\mathbf{V}
satisfies the ordered local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
if and only if it satisfies the GMP for
\mathcal{G}
.
∎

Theorem
2

(Unique AC for each CI Invoked by C-LMP)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph,
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
