a consistent ordering, and
X
a variable in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
For every conditional independence relation invoked by the c-component local Markov property of the form
X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}
, there is exactly one ancestral c-component
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X}
such that
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus((\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{})\cup\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{})
and
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
.

Proof.

The result follows from Def.
5
, Lemma
B.3.1
, and Cor.
B.3.1
.
∎

Proposition
1

(Number of CIs Invoked by C-LMP)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
n
and
s\leq n
denote the number of variables and the size of the largest c-component in
\mathcal{G}
respectively. Then, the c-component local Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
with respect to
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
invokes
O(n2^{s})
conditional independencies implied by
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathbf{V}
.
Moreover, there exists a graph
\mathcal{G}
and a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
for which the property induces
\Omega(2^{n})
conditional independencies.

Proof.

By Def.
5
, the set of CIs invoked by C-LMP for a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
is in bijection with the set of ACs,
\mathcal{AC}_{X}
.
Therefore, it suffices to bound
|\mathcal{AC}_{X}|
. Recall that
\mathcal{AC}_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}})
(where
\mathcal{P}(\cdot)
denotes the power-set operation). Then,
|\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}}|\leq s\implies|\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}})|\leq 2^{s}\implies|\mathcal{AC}_{X}|\leq 2^{s}.
Total number of CIs
k
invoked by C-LMP for all variables is thus
k\leq n2^{s}\in O(n2^{s})
.

Next, consider the graph
\mathcal{G}
shown in Fig.
B.4.2
for which C-LMP invokes
\Omega(2^{n})
CIs.

Fix
V_{i},i\in[k]
. For each
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\{V_{j}\}_{j<i}
, we get an AAC
\{V_{i},X,Z\}\cup\mathbf{C}
relative to
V_{i}
inducing the CI:
V_{i}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{Y\}\mid\{X,Z\}\cup\mathbf{C}
(The definition of admissibility of AC is given by Def.
6
). There are
2^{i-1}
such CIs for each
V_{i}
. Then, the total number of CIs across all such
V_{i}
is

\sum_{i=1}^{k}2^{i-1}=2^{k-1}-1=2^{n-4}-1\in\Omega(2^{n})

Since, for any
\mathcal{G}
, we have that
s\leq n
, the upper bound
O(n2^{s})
is thus tight ignoring the linear term in
n
.
∎

C.2
Section
4
Proofs

Notation.
For the proofs in this section, given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
defined on a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
, and variables
X,Y\in\mathbf{V}
, we use
X\sim Y
to denote an arbitrary path (possibly of length 0, when
X=Y
) between
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
;
X\rightsquigarrow Y
to denote a directed path (possibly of length 0, when
X=Y
) from
X
to
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
; and
X\circ\rightarrow Y
to denote that there is either an edge
X\rightarrow Y
or
X\leftrightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition C.2.1

(Time Complexity of Computing a C-component)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
over a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
and a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}
, the c-component
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}}
containing
X
in
\mathcal{G}
is computable in time
O(n+m)
, where
n
and
m
are the numbers of nodes and edges in
\mathcal{G}
respectively.

Proof.

Using breadth-first search (BFS), compute the set of nodes reachable from the starting node
X
by following only bidirected edges. This takes time
O(n+m)
, the complexity of BFS.
∎

1:

function

IsAdmissible
(
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{C}
)

2:

Output:
True if a given AC
\mathbf{C}
relative to
X
is admissible; False otherwise.

3:

\mathbf{S}^{+}\leftarrow\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}

4:

\mathbf{W}\leftarrow\mathbf{S}^{+}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}

5:

if

\mathbf{W}\neq\emptyset

then

6:

return
True

7:

else

8:

return
False

9:

end

function

Figure C.2.1:
A function that checks if a given AC is admissible.

Lemma C.2.1

(Correctness of
IsAdmissible
)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}}
, a variable
X
, and a set of variables
\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
, let
\mathbf{C}
be an ancestral c-component relative to
X
.
Then,
IsAdmissible
returns True if
\mathbf{C}
is admissible, and False otherwise.

IsAdmissible
takes
O(n+m)
time where
n
and
m
represent the number of nodes and edges in
\mathcal{G}
, respectively.

Proof.

For correctness, it immediately follows from Def.
5
and Def.
6
.

IsAdmissible
runs in
O(n+m)
time since the construction of the sets
\mathbf{S}^{+}
and
\mathbf{W}
takes
O(n+m)
time for each set.
∎

Lemma C.2.2

(Existence of a Separator)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
, let
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
be sets of nodes with
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{R}\cap(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})=\emptyset
. If there exists a set
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
separating
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{Z_{0}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
, then
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\cap\mathbf{R}
is such a set.

Proof.

Assume there exists
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
separating
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
such that
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{Z_{0}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
. For some
X\in\mathbf{X}
,
Y\in\mathbf{Y}
, consider a path
\pi
from
X
to
Y
in
\mathcal{G}
, consisting of nodes
\{X=V_{0},V_{1},\dots,V_{n},V_{n+1}=Y\}
where
V_{i},V_{i}+1
are adjacent in
\mathcal{G}
for
0\leq i\leq n
. Note that we must have
n\geq 1
; otherwise,
X,Y
are adjacent and cannot be separated.

If none of the variables
V_{i},i\in[n]
is a collider, then each
V_{i}
must be in
\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{X,Y\}}\right)\mathclose{}
. If
V_{i}\not\in\mathbf{R}
for any
i\in[n]
, then
V_{i}\not\in\mathbf{Z_{0}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
for any
i\in[n]
, and hence
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
does not block
\pi
, which is a contradiction. Therefore, there exists
V_{i}
such that
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\cap\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{Z}
and hence
\mathbf{Z}
blocks
\pi
.

If some
V_{i}
is a collider, let
\mathbf{C}=\{C_{i_{1}},\dots,C_{i_{k}}\}\subseteq\{V_{1},\dots,V_{n}\}
denote the set of colliders on
\pi
such that
i_{j}<i_{j+1}
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
. If there is a variable
C\in\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{Z}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{C\}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}=\emptyset
(in other words,
\mathbf{Z}
does not contain
C
or any of its descendants), then
\mathbf{Z}
blocks
\pi
due to the inactive collider
C
. Otherwise, consider the case that
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
i.e. for every
C\in\mathbf{C}
, either
C
is in
\mathbf{Z}
or some descendant of
C
is in
\mathbf{Z}
, and hence
C
is active (when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. Since
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\cap\mathbf{R}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, we have
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\implies\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
. For any
V_{i}
in
\pi
, either
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
or
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{X,Y\}}\right)\mathclose{}
or
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
; therefore,
\{V_{i}\}_{1:n}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{X,Y\}\cup\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
. Hence,
\pi
is blocked by
\mathbf{Z}
unless every
V_{i}\in\mathbf{R}
is a collider; that is,
\{V_{i}\}_{1:n}\cap\mathbf{R}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
. Assume toward contradiction that
\{V_{i}\}_{1:n}\cap\mathbf{R}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
.

We show, by induction on the index
i_{j},j\in[k]
of
\mathbf{C}
, that for every
j\in[k]
, there exists a variable
X_{0}\in\mathbf{X}
such that there is an active
X_{0}\sim C_{i_{k}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j}+1}
path when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
.

Base case
. Consider
C_{i_{1}}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
. The sub-path of
\pi
from
X
to
C_{i_{1}}
is unblocked by
\mathbf{Z_{0}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
. This is because for any node
V
on this sub-path (excluding
X
and
C_{i_{1}}
),
V\not\in\mathbf{C}
by assumption and
\{V_{i}\}_{1:n}\cap\mathbf{R}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\implies V\not\in\mathbf{R}\implies V\not\in\mathbf{Z}_{0}
.

•

If
C_{i_{1}}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Y}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, there is a directed path
\pi^{\prime}
from
C_{i_{1}}
to
Y^{\prime}
for some
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Y}
. The
X\sim C_{i_{1}}
sub-path of
\pi
(which is unblocked by
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
), adjoined with
\pi^{\prime}
, gives an active path from
X\in\mathbf{X}
to
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Y}
. For
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
to block this path, it must block
\pi^{\prime}
. Hence,
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
contains a descendant of
C_{i_{1}}
and
C_{i_{1}}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
, giving an active sub-path of
\pi
,
X\sim V_{i_{1}-1}\circ\rightarrow C_{i_{1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{1}+1}
.

•

If
C_{i_{1}}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, there is a directed path
\pi^{\prime}
from
C_{i_{1}}
to
X^{\prime}
for some
X^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}
. If
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
contains a descendant of
C_{i_{1}}
, then
C_{i_{1}}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
. Therefore, the sub-path of
\pi
,
X\sim V_{i_{1}-1}\circ\rightarrow C_{i_{1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{1}+1}
is active. If
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
contains no descendants of
C_{i_{1}}
, then
\pi^{\prime}
is unblocked by
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
, giving an active
X^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}}C_{i_{1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{1}+1}
path.

•

If
C_{i_{1}}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, since
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{Z_{0}}
, we condition on a descendant of
C_{i_{1}}
and
C_{i_{1}}
is active, giving an active sub-path of
\pi
,
X\sim V_{i_{1}-1}\circ\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{i_{1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{1}+1}
.

Inductive assumption.
Assume for some
j\in[k-1]
, there is an active
X_{0}\sim\mathbf{C}_{i_{j}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j}+1}
path for some
X_{0}\in\mathbf{X}
.

Inductive step.
We show this implies the existence of an active
X_{0}^{\prime}\sim\mathbf{C}_{i_{j+1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j+1}+1}
path for some
X_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}
. Note that the sub-path of
\pi
from
C_{i_{j}}
to
C_{i_{j+1}}
is unblocked by
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
. This is because for any node
V
on this sub-path (excluding
C_{j}
and
C_{j+1}
),
V\not\in\mathbf{C}
by assumption and
\{V_{i}\}_{1:n}\cap\mathbf{R}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\implies V\not\in\mathbf{R}\implies V\not\in\mathbf{Z}_{0}
.

•

If
C_{i_{j+1}}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Y}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, there is a directed path
\pi^{\prime}:C_{i_{j+1}}\rightsquigarrow Y^{\prime}
for some
Y^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Y}
. By the inductive assumption, we get an active path
X_{0}\sim\mathbf{C}_{i_{j}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j}+1}\circ\rightarrow C_{i_{j+1}}\rightsquigarrow Y^{\prime}
. For
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
to block this path, it must block
\pi^{\prime}
. Hence,
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
contains a descendant of
C_{i_{j+1}}
and
C_{i_{j+1}}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
, giving an active

X_{0}\sim C_{i_{j}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j}+1}\sim V_{i_{j+1}-1}\circ\rightarrow C_{i_{j+1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j+1}+1}
path.

•

If
C_{i_{j+1}}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, there is a directed path
\pi^{\prime}
from
C_{i_{j+1}}
to
X^{\prime}
for some
X^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}
. If
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
contains a descendant of
C_{i_{j+1}}
, then
C_{i_{j+1}}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
. Using the inductive assumption, we get an active path
X_{0}\sim C_{i_{j}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j}+1}\sim V_{i_{j+1}-1}\circ\rightarrow C_{i_{j+1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j+1}+1}
. If
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
contains no descendants of
C_{i_{j+1}}
, then
\pi^{\prime}
is unblocked by
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
, giving an active path
X^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}}C_{i_{j+1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j+1}+1}
path.

•

If
C_{i_{j+1}}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}
, since
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{Z_{0}}
, we condition on a descendant of
C_{i_{j+1}}
and
C_{i_{j+1}}
is active. Using the inductive assumption, we get an active path
X_{0}\sim C_{i_{j}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j}+1}\sim V_{i_{j+1}-1}\circ\rightarrow C_{i_{j+1}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{j+1}+1}
.

By induction, we have an active
X_{0}\sim C_{i_{k}}\leftarrow\circ V_{i_{k}+1}
path for some
X_{0}\in\mathbf{X}
. The
V_{i_{k}+1}\sim Y
sub-path of
\pi
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
. This is because for any node
V
on this sub-path (excluding
V_{i_{k}+1}
and
Y
),
V\not\in\mathbf{C}
by assumption and
\{V_{i}\}_{1:n}\cap\mathbf{R}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\implies V\not\in\mathbf{R}\implies V\not\in\mathbf{Z}_{0}
. Recall that by assumption,
V_{i_{k}+1}
is not a collider. We thus have an
X_{0}\sim Y
path which is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z_{0}}
. We thus have a contradiction.
∎

1:

function

FindSeparator
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
)

2:

Output:
A set of variables
\mathbf{Z}

d
-separating
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
under the constraint
\mathbf{I}\setminus(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})\subseteq\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathbf{R}\setminus(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})
if such
\mathbf{Z}
exists;
\perp
otherwise.

3:

\mathbf{R^{\prime}}\leftarrow\mathbf{R}\setminus(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})

4:

\mathbf{Z}\leftarrow\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\cap\mathbf{R}^{\prime}

5:

if

\mathbf{Z}

d
-separates
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}

then

6:

return

\mathbf{Z}

7:

else

8:

return

\perp

9:

end

function

Figure C.2.2:
A function that finds a separator of a given pair of sets of variables, if it exists.

Lemma C.2.3

(Correctness of
FindSeparator
)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
, let
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
be sets of nodes with
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
.
FindSeparator
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
) has a non-empty output if and only if there exists a set
\mathbf{Z}
separating
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathbf{I}\setminus(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})\subseteq\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathbf{R}\setminus(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})
. Moreover, any output
\mathbf{Z}\neq\perp
satisfies
\mathbf{X}\perp_{\mathcal{G}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{I}\setminus(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})\subseteq\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathbf{R}\setminus(\mathbf{X}\cup\mathbf{Y})
. Finally,
FindSeparator
runs in time
O(n+m)
, where
n
and
m
are the numbers of nodes and edges respectively in
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

The correctness is immediate from the construction of
FindSeparator
and Lemma
C.2.2
. For the runtime, constructing
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{Z}
in the algorithm takes time
O(n)
and
O(n+m)
respectively.
Verifying whether
\mathbf{Z}

d
-separates
\mathbf{X}
from
\mathbf{Y}
in
\mathcal{G}
or not, as shown in line
5
, may be performed by using the Bayes-Ball algorithm
(
Shachter 2013
)
on a modified graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{G}
where
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is constructed as follows: start from
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}
, and replace each edge
X\leftrightarrow Y
with an explicit latent common cause
X\leftarrow U_{XY}\rightarrow Y
.
The construction of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
takes
O(n+m)
time, and the Bayes-Ball algorithm runs in
O(n+m)
time.
The overall runtime of
FindSeparator
is thus
O(n+m)
.
∎

Since the size of the input graph
\mathcal{G}
is
O(n+m)
,
FindSeparator
is asymptotically optimal.

Lemma
1

(Correctness of
FindAAC
)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
, a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, and a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
be ancestral c-components relative to
X
such that
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
.
FindAAC
(
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
) outputs an admissible ancestral c-component
\mathbf{C}
relative to
X
such that
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
if such a
\mathbf{C}
exists, and
\perp
otherwise.

Proof.

By assumption,
\mathbf{I}
is an AC relative to
X
in the desired range since
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
.
FindAAC
outputs
\mathbf{I}
(at line
4
) if and only if
\mathbf{I}
is admissible. This follows from the correctness of
IsAdmissible
(by Lemma
C.2.1
).

Assume
\mathbf{I}
is not admissible. It remains to show that there exists an AAC
\mathbf{C_{0}}
relative to
X
such that
\mathbf{I}\subsetneq\mathbf{C_{0}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
if and only if there exists a variable
D\in\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
and a set
\mathbf{Z}
such that
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X,D\}\subseteq\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X,D\}
and
X\perp_{\mathcal{G}}D\mid\mathbf{Z}
. Moreover, the output of
FindAAC
at line
8
must be an AAC relative to
X
in the given range.

(
\implies
) Since
\mathbf{I}
is not admissible, by Def.
6
we have

\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus(\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{})=\emptyset

(16)

Since
\mathbf{C_{0}}\supsetneq\mathbf{I}
is admissible, by Def.
6
we have

\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus(\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{})\neq\emptyset

(17)

However,
\mathbf{I}\subsetneq\mathbf{C_{0}}\implies\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Therefore, Eq. (
16
) and Eq. (
17
) imply that there exists a variable
D\in\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
such that
D\in\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
and
d\not\in\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
.
By the definition of C-LMP (shown in Def.
5
), we have that
X\perp_{\mathcal{G}}D\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
.
Since
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{C_{0}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
and
D\not\in\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we have
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X,D\}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X,D\}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{Z}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{0}}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
is a set such that
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X,D\}\subseteq\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X,D\}
and
X\perp_{\mathcal{G}}D\mid\mathbf{Z}
.
The correctness of
FindSeparator
(Lemma
C.2.3
) implies that
FindAAC
detects the existence of
\mathbf{Z}
and outputs
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
at line
8
. In the proof for the reverse direction, we will show that
\mathbf{C}
thus defined is in fact admissible.

(
\impliedby
) Consider some
D\in\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
such that

\begin{split}\mathbf{Z}&=\textsc{FindSeparator}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},\{X\},\{D\},Pa(\mathbf{I}),Pa(\mathbf{R}))\\
&\neq\perp\end{split}

By the correctness of
FindSeparator
(Lemma
C.2.3
), we have
X\perp_{\mathcal{G}}D\mid\mathbf{Z}
. We give a constructive proof of existence by showing that
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
is an AAC relative to
X
such that
\mathbf{I}\subsetneq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
.

Clearly,
\mathbf{C}
is an AC by construction and
\mathbf{I}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{I}}}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbf{I},\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}\subseteq\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}

(18)

\displaystyle\implies\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}

(19)

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}}}

(20)

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}

(21)

where the last implication follows since
\mathbf{R}
is an AC relative to
X
by assumption, implying that
\mathbf{R}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{R}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
.
Moreover, we claim that
\mathbf{C}
is admissible, i.e.,

\mathbf{S}^{+}=\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\setminus(\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{})\neq\emptyset

We will show that
D\not\in\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
, hence
\mathbf{S}^{+}
contains
D
(and is therefore non-empty). We know
D\in\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}
. Assume, towards contradiction, that
D\in\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\cup\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Note that
\mathbf{I}\setminus\{X,D\}\subseteq\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}\cup\{X,D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}\cup\{X,D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Since
X,D
are non-adjacent (because
\mathbf{Z}
separates them), this implies the existence of a path
\pi
of one of the following types:

1.

If
D\in\mathbf{C}
, then
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}\dots\leftrightarrow V_{n}\leftrightarrow V_{n+1}=D
with
n\geq 1
and each
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}\cup\{X,D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
for
i\in[n+1]

2.

If
D\in\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}\dots\leftrightarrow V_{n}\leftarrow V_{n+1}=D
with
n\geq 1
and each
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
for
i\in[n+1]

3.

If
D\in\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{})
, then
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow V_{n}\leftrightarrow V_{n+1}=A\rightsquigarrow D
with each
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}\cup\{X,D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
for each
i\in[n+1]
with
n\geq 0
and
A\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
. It is possible that the path
A\rightsquigarrow d
has length 0, i.e.,
A=D
.

We show by induction that there is an active
X\sim V_{i}\leftarrow\circ V_{i+1}
path for each
i\in[n]
when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
.

Base case.
We know
V_{1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}\cup\{X,D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
.

•

If
V_{1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
,
V_{1}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
, hence the sub-path
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}\leftarrow\circ V_{2}
of
\pi
is active.

•

If
V_{1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then there is a path
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}\rightsquigarrow D
. Since
\mathbf{Z}
must block this path, we have
\mathbf{Z}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{1}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\neq\emptyset
, hence
V_{1}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
and the sub-path
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}\leftarrow\circ V_{2}
of
\pi
is active.

•

If
V_{1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{X\}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then
X\in\mathbf{I}
,
V_{1}\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\{X\}}\right)\mathclose{}
, and
\mathbf{I}
is an AC implies that
V_{1}\in\mathbf{I}
. Since
V_{1}\not\in\{X,D\}
, this implies that
V_{1}\in\mathbf{Z}
. Therefore,
V_{1}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
and the sub-path
X\leftrightarrow V_{1}\leftarrow\circ V_{2}
of
\pi
is active.

Inductive assumption
. Assume, for some
i\in[n]
, there is an active
X\sim V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}
path when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
.

Inductive step.
We show that there is an active
X\sim V_{i+1}\leftarrow\circ V_{i+2}
path when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. We know
V_{i+1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}\cup\{X,D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
.

•

If
V_{i+1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
,
V_{i+1}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. Therefore, the inductive assumption gives us an active path
X\sim V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}\leftarrow\circ V_{i+2}
when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
.

•

If
V_{i+1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{D\}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then there is a path
V_{i+1}\rightsquigarrow D
. By the inductive assumption, there is an active path
X\sim V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}
when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. Since
\mathbf{Z}
must block the path
X\sim V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}\rightsquigarrow D
, we have
\mathbf{Z}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{i+1}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\neq\emptyset
, hence
V_{i+1}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
and the path
X\sim V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}\leftarrow\circ V_{i+2}
is active.

•

If
V_{i+1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{X\}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then there is a path
X\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}}V_{i+1}
. If
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{i+1}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{Z}\neq\emptyset
, then
V_{i+1}
is active when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
and by the inductive assumption, the path
X\sim V_{i}\leftrightarrow V_{i+1}\leftarrow\circ V_{i+2}
is active. If
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{i+1}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{Z}=\emptyset
, then the path
X\mathrel{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}}V_{i+1}\leftarrow\circ V_{i+2}
is active.

Therefore, by induction, there is an active
X\sim V_{n}\leftarrow\circ V_{n+1}
path when conditioning on
\mathbf{Z}
. If
V_{n+1}=D
, this contradicts
X\perp_{\mathcal{G}}d\mid\mathbf{Z}
. Otherwise, if
V_{n+1}=A\rightsquigarrow D
in Case (3), then
\mathbf{Z}
must block the path
A\rightsquigarrow D
.
This implies that
A\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}
; moreover,
A\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
and
\mathbf{C}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
implies that
A\in\mathbf{C}
, which contradicts the assumption that
A\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
.
∎

Proposition C.2.2

(Runtime of
FindAAC
)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
, a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, and a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
be ancestral c-components relative to
X
such that
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
.

FindAAC
(
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
) runs in
O(n(n+m))
time where
n
and
m
denote the numbers of nodes and edges in
\mathcal{G}
respectively.

Proof.

A call to the function
IsAdmissible
in line
3
takes
O(n+m)
time (by Lemma
C.2.1
).

FindAAC
computes a set of variables
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}
(shown in line
5
) only once, which takes
O(n+m)
time. There are at most
O(n)
iterations of the for loop, within which a call to the function
FindSeparator
(by Lemma
C.2.3
) and the construction of a c-component
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}\cup\mathbf{Z}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
in line
8
(by Prop.
C.2.1
) take time
O(n+m)
. Thus, the total runtime of
FindAAC
is
O(n(n+m))
.
∎

Proposition C.2.3

(Ancestrality of Modified ACs)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
, a consistent ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, and a variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, let
\mathbf{C}
be an ancestral c-component relative to
X
. For any
\mathbf{S}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
such that
X\not\in\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
,
\mathbf{C_{S}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
is an ancestral c-component relative to
X
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\mathbf{C_{S}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
. Since
\mathbf{C_{S}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}}
and
\mathbf{C_{S}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{C_{S}}}}
, we have
\mathbf{C_{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
. To show
\mathbf{C_{S}}\supseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
, we make use of two facts.
Since
\mathbf{C_{S}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
, we have
\mathbf{C_{S}}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
. This further implies that
\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
(if some
W\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
, then
\exists S\in\mathbf{S}
such that
S\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{W\}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}
contradicts
\mathbf{C_{S}}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
). Therefore, we have
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
; Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
. We now show that
\mathbf{C_{S}}\supseteq\mathbf{A}
. Consider some variable
W\in\mathbf{A}
. Then, there exists a variable
Y\in\mathbf{C_{S}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
such that
W\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{Y\}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Moreover, since
Y\in\mathbf{C_{S}}\subseteq\mathbf{A}
, we either have
W=Y
(and hence
W\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
) or a path
W=V_{k}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow V_{1}\leftrightarrow Y
for some
k\geq 1
with
V_{i}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
for each
i\in[k]
(by the construction of
\mathbf{A}
). We show by induction that
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
for each
i\in[k]
.

Base case
.
k=1
. Since
V_{1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we have
\{V_{1}\}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
. Moreover,
V_{1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
(since
\mathbf{C_{S}}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
). Furthermore,
Y\in\mathbf{C_{S}}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
. So,
Y\in\mathbf{C}
,
V_{1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
,
V_{1}\leftrightarrow Y
, and
\mathbf{C}
is an AC by assumption implies that
V_{1}\in\mathbf{C}
. Therefore,
V_{1}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Since
Y\in\mathbf{C_{S}}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
,
Y\leftrightarrow V_{1}
, and
V_{1}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we get
V_{1}\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
.

Inductive assumption
. Assume, for some
i\in[k-1]
, we have
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
.

Inductive step
. By similar reasoning as in the base case, we show that
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
. Since
V_{i+1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we have
\{V_{i+1}\}\cap\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}=\emptyset
. Moreover,
V_{i+1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C_{S}}}\right)\mathclose{}\subseteq\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Furthermore, by the inductive assumption,
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C_{S}}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
. So,
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
,
V_{i+1}\in\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
,
V_{i+1}\leftrightarrow V_{i}
, and
\mathbf{C}
being an AC implies that
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{C}
. Therefore,
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Since
V_{i}\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
,
V_{i+1}\leftrightarrow V_{i}
, and
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{S}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we get
V_{i+1}\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
.

Therefore,
W\in\mathbf{C_{S}}
and since
W
was chosen arbitrarily from
\mathbf{A}
, we have
\mathbf{A}\subseteq\mathbf{C_{S}}
.
∎

Lemma
2

(Correctness of
ListCIX
)
.

ListCIX
(
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
) enumerates all and only all non-vacuous conditional independence relations invoked by the c-component local Markov property associated with
X
and admissible ancestral c-components
\mathbf{C}
relative to
X
where
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
.
Further,
ListCIX
runs in
O(n^{2}(n+m))
delay where
n
and
m
represent the number of nodes and edges in
\mathcal{G}
, respectively.

Proof.

We show the correctness of
ListCIX
and the running time that
ListCIX
runs in
O(n^{2}(n+m))
delay.

•

Correctness:
We prove correctness by structural induction on the binary recursion tree for
ListCIX
, rooted at
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R})
. We claim that
ListCIX
called at a node
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
enumerates all and only non-vacuous CIs of
X
invoked by C-LMP (Def.
5
) such that the conditioning set is of the form
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
for some AC
\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
.

Base case.
Consider a leaf node
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})

7
7

7

A leaf node is a node that has no children.
. Let

\mathbf{C}:=\textsc{FindAAC}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}).

Since we are at a leaf node, we either have

1.

\mathbf{C}=\perp
, in which case
ListCIX
outputs nothing at
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
. By the correctness of
FindAAC
(Lemma
1
) there are no CIs of
X
invoked by C-LMP such that the conditioning set is of the form
\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{X\}
for some AC
\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the output is correct.

2.

\mathbf{I}^{\prime}=\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
and hence
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{I}^{\prime}
. Similarly, by the correctness of
FindAAC
and the definition of C-LMP,
ListCIX
outputs the unique non-vacuous CI of the desired form at
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
.

Note that these are the only conditions under which we are at a leaf node. If
\mathbf{C}\neq\perp
and
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cap(\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathbf{I}^{\prime})
must be non-empty (because
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
is a c-component such that
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\supsetneq\mathbf{I}^{\prime}
) and we recurse.

Inductive assumption.
Assume the claim holds for some nodes
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}),

\mathcal{N}_{2}(\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2})
.

Inductive step.
We show that the claim holds for any node
\mathcal{N}_{0}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
whose children are
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}),\mathcal{N}_{2}(\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2})
. We first define three collections of ACs relative to
X
. Recall that
\mathcal{AC}_{X}
(Def.
A.1.4
) denotes the set of all ACs relative to
X
.

\begin{split}\mathcal{AC}_{0}=\{\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\mid\>&\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X},\mathbf{C}\text{ is AAC, }\\
&\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\}\end{split}

\begin{split}\mathcal{AC}_{1}=\{\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\mid\>&\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X},\mathbf{C}\text{ is AAC, }\\
&\mathbf{I}_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{1}\}\end{split}

\begin{split}\mathcal{AC}_{2}=\{\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}\mid\>&\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{X},\mathbf{C}\text{ is AAC, }\\
&\mathbf{I}_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{2}\}\end{split}

It suffices to show that
\mathcal{AC}_{0}=\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}
, since this implies that any CI that should be output by
ListCIX
at
\mathcal{N}_{0}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
is output by
ListCIX
at either
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1})
or
\mathcal{N}_{2}(\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2})
. Since
ListCIX
at
\mathcal{N}_{0}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
calls
ListCIX
at both
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}(\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2})
, we prove the claim.

By construction at lines
10
-
11
, since
\mathcal{N}_{0}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
has children
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}(\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2})
, we have (without loss of generality) that
(\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1})=(\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\cup\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}}},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2})=(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}}})
for some
S\in\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\cap(\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathbf{I}^{\prime})
.

First, some technicalities. we want to show that
(\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}),(\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2})
are well-defined and non-vacuous inputs to
ListCIX
.

–

Since
S\in\mathbf{T}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
is an AC relative to
X
, we have
\mathbf{I}_{1}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\cup\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}=\mathbf{R}_{1}
. Furthermore,
\mathbf{I}_{1}
is an AC by construction.

–

Since
S\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathbf{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}
is an AC, we have
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{I}^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Since
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
, this further implies that
\mathbf{I}_{2}=\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{2}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{R}_{2}
is an AC by Prop.
C.2.3
.

We show the equality of
\mathcal{AC}_{0}
and
\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}
in both directions.

–

\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{AC}_{0}

For any
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}
, we have either

*

\mathbf{I}_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{1}
and hence
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
since
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}=\mathbf{R}_{1}
,
or

*

\mathbf{I}_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{2}
and hence
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
since
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}=\mathbf{I}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
.

Therefore,
\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{AC}_{0}
.

–

\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}\supseteq\mathcal{AC}_{0}

For any
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{0}
, we have
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
. Then, we have either

*

S\in\mathbf{C}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\cup\{S\}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
. Since
\mathbf{C}
is an AC, we must have
\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\cup\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
(otherwise, there is an ancestor of
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\cup\{S\}
not in
\mathbf{C}
which is connected by a bi-directed path to some node in
\mathbf{I}\cup\{S\}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
). Therefore, we have
\mathbf{I}_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{1}=\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
and hence
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{1}
.

*

S\not\in\mathbf{C}
. Since
\mathbf{C}
is an AC, this implies
\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}\cap\mathbf{C}=\emptyset
. Moreover,
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
further implies that
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\setminus\mathit{De}\mathopen{}\left({\{S\}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
. Therefore, we have
\mathbf{I}_{2}=\mathbf{I}_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{AC}_{2}
.

Therefore,
\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}\supseteq\mathcal{AC}_{0}
.

Hence,
\mathcal{AC}_{0}=\mathcal{AC}_{1}\cup\mathcal{AC}_{2}
and we are done. Since
ListCIX
enumerates all AACs relative to
X
correctly, by Thm.
2
, it enumerates all non-vacuous CIs invoked by C-LMP for
X
correctly.

•

Running time:

Consider the recursion tree for
ListCIX
.
Whenever a tree node
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{I}^{\prime},\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
is visited, the function
FindAAC
is called, which takes
O(n(n+m))
time (by Lemma
C.2
).
If
FindAAC
outputs
\perp
, then
ListCIX
does not search further from
\mathcal{N}
because there exists no AAC
\mathbf{C}
relative to
X
with
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, recursion continues until a leaf tree node is visited.
In each level of the tree, a single node
S
is removed from
\mathbf{T}
.
Either the variable
S
, is added to
\mathbf{I}
, resulting in
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}
given at line
9
(by
S\in\mathit{Sp}\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{I}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\mathbf{I}
), or the variable
S
is removed from
\mathbf{R}
to construct
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
which is shown at line
9
.
Any
\mathbf{C}
is uniquely contained in one child; therefore, no CI is output more than once.
The depth of the tree is at most
n
, and the time required to find one
\mathbf{C}
and output one non-vacuous CI invoked by C-LMP associated with
\mathbf{C}
(following Def.
5
), is
O(n^{2}(n+m))
.
In the worst case scenario,
n
branches will be aborted (i.e.,
FindAAC
outputs
\perp
on every level of the tree) before reaching the first leaf.
It takes
O(n^{2}(n+m))
time to produce either the first output or halt.

∎

Theorem
3

(Correctness of
ListCI
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph and
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
a consistent ordering.

ListCI
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
) enumerates all and only all non-vacuous conditional independence relations invoked by the c-component local Markov property in
O(n^{2}(n+m))
delay where
n
and
m
represent the number of nodes and edges in
\mathcal{G}
, respectively.

Proof.

We show the correctness of
ListCI
and the running time that
ListCI
runs in
O(n^{2}(n+m))
delay.

•

Correctness:

ListCI
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
) iterates over each variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.
For each
X
,
ListCI
constructs two ACs relative to
X
:
\mathbf{I}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathit{An}\mathopen{}\left({\{X\}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
and
\mathbf{R}=\mathcal{C}(X)_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}}}
.

\mathbf{I}
is the minimum-size AC relative to
X
and
\mathbf{R}
is the maximum-size AC relative to
X
.
Then,
ListCI
calls the function
ListCIX
(
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
) that outputs all and only all non-vacuous CIs invoked by C-LMP associated with
X
, which is performed by generating all and only all AACs
\mathbf{C}
relative to
X
under the constraint
\mathbf{I}\subseteq\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{R}
(by Lemma
2
).

ListCI
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
) iterates over each variable
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
, and thus
ListCI
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
) lists all and only all non-vacuous CIs invoked by C-LMP.

•

Running time:

There are two types of worst case scenarios.

1.

No CI is invoked by C-LMP.

By the assumption that no CI is invoked by C-LMP, Def.
5
and Def.
6
, none of the ACs
\mathbf{C}
relative to
X
for any
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
is admissible.
For each
X
visited by
ListCI
(
\mathcal{G},\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
),
ListCI
calls the function
ListCIX
(
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
) at line
5
.

ListCIX
spends
O(n(n+m))
time and terminates with no output since
ListCIX
calls the function
\textsc{FindAAC}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R})
at line
3
, which returns
\perp
(by Lemma
1
).

ListCI
checks the next variable of
X
, if any exists.
Since
|\mathbf{V}^{\prec}|=n
,
ListCI
spends
O(n^{2}(n+m))
time and then terminates with no output.

2.

No CI invoked by C-LMP exists for all variables
X\in\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
except for the last variable
X_{n}
in the ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.

For the first
n-1
variables in
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
,
ListCI
spends
O(n^{2}(n+m))=O(n(n+m)*(n-1))
time producing no output.
More specifically, for each variable,
ListCIX
spends
O(n(n+m))
time and terminates with no output since
ListCIX
calls
\textsc{FindAAC}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R})
at line
3
, which returns
\perp
.
When
X=X_{n}
,
ListCI
calls the function
ListCIX
(
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq X}},X,\mathbf{V}^{\leq X},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{R}
) at line
5
where
ListCIX
spends
O(n^{2}(n+m))
time to output one non-vacuous CI invoked by C-LMP that is associated with
X_{n}
.
In total,
ListCI
spends
O(n^{2}(n+m))
time to produce an output.

∎

C.3
Appendix Proofs

Proposition C.3.1

(Total Number of CIs Invoked by GMP)
.

Given a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
over a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
with
n=|\mathbf{V}|
, the global Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
invokes
O(4^{n})
number of conditional independence relations. Moreover, there exists a causal graph
\mathcal{G}
for which the bound is tight, that is, the global Markov property for
\mathcal{G}
implies
\Omega(4^{n})
number of conditional independence relations.

Proof.

Each CI invoked by GMP is given by a choice of disjoint sets
\mathbf{X,Y,Z\subseteq V}
where
\mathbf{X,Y}\neq\emptyset
and a
d
-separation statement
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
. The number of such statements is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle d(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n}{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n-i}\binom{n-i}{j}\sum_{k=0}^{n-i-j}\binom{n-i-j}{k}

(22)

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{n}(2^{n}-1)}{2}-3^{n}+2^{n}

(23)

where we divide the quantity by 2 to avoid double-counting the following two symmetrical statements:
\mathbf{X}\perp_{d}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{Y}\perp_{d}\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Z}
(since
d
-separation is symmetric). We first simplify the inner-most sum.

\sum_{k=0}^{n-i-j}\binom{n-i-j}{k}=2^{n-i-j}

(24)

This gives

d(n)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n}{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n-i}\binom{n-i}{j}2^{n-i-j}

(25)

We then simplify the second nested sum. Note that by the binomial theorem,

\displaystyle 3^{n-i}

\displaystyle=(2+1)^{n-i}

(26)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n-i}\binom{n-i}{j}2^{n-i-j}1^{j}

(27)

\displaystyle=2^{n-i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-i}\binom{n-i}{j}1^{j}2^{n-i-j}

(28)

Therefore,

\displaystyle d(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n}{i}(3^{n-i}-2^{n-i})

(29)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n}{i}3^{n-i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n}{i}2^{n-i})\right)

(30)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(4^{n}+2^{n}\right)-3^{n}\in O(4^{n}).

(31)

Moreover, for
\mathcal{G}
given by the independent set on
n
variables (i.e.,
\mathcal{G}
contains no edges), every possible
d
-separation holds, therefore we get
d(n)\in\Omega(4^{n})
number of CIs implied by GMP.
∎

Appendix D
Discussion and Examples

D.1
Explaining Markov properties

A causal DAG on
n
variables may encode
\Theta(4^{n})
CIs (Prop.
C.3.1
).
In this section, we explain how a subset of these CIs, often considered a ‘basis’
(
Bareinboim et al. 2022
)
, may imply all others.

The CI relation is a
semi-graphoid

(
Pearl 1986
;
Pearl 1988
)
.
Given an arbitrary probability distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
over a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
, CIs in
P(\mathbf{v})
must exhibit certain properties.
Specifically, for disjoint sets of variables
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{W},\mathbf{Z}
, where
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\neq\emptyset
, the probability axioms can be used to show that the following properties hold:

1.

Symmetry:
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}\iff\mathbf{Y}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Z}

2.

Decomposition:
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}

3.

Weak union:
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}\cup\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}\cup\mathbf{Y}

4.

Contraction:
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\mid\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}\cup\mathbf{Y}\implies\mathbf{X}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{Y}\cup\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}

We give an example to show how these axioms can be applied.

◼ Example D.1.1
.

The DAG
\mathcal{G}
(Fig.
1(a)
) encodes 5 CIs, but the colored subsets of CIs can be used to derive all others, as shown in Fig.
1(b)
.
For one example, the CI
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
implies all others.
In the context of testing
\mathcal{G}
against observational data, it suffices to test only
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
.
For another example,
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{2}
and
X_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{4}\mid X_{2}
together imply
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
by the contraction axiom, and hence all other CIs.
Therefore, it suffices also to only test
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{2}
and
X_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{4}\mid X_{2}
.
∎

In the given example, scrutiny revealed which CIs are sufficient to derive others via the semi-graphoid axioms.
Markov properties, however, provide a systematic way to identify such CIs.
The semi-graphoid axioms can be used to show equivalence between Markov properties: for example, between the local Markov property and GMP for Markovian DAGs
(
Pearl 1988
;
Lauritzen et al. 1990
;
Lauritzen 1996
)
, and between (LMP,
\prec
) and GMP for semi-Markovian DAGs
(
Richardson 2003
)
.

D.2
C-LMP and the Semi-Markov Factorisation

In this section, we develop a connection between Markov properties and a related notion of compatibility between causal graphs and observational data – the factorisation that the distribution should admit.
This offers another perspective on the combinatorial explosion in the number of CIs invoked by C-LMP in the semi-Markovian case, compared with the Markovian case.

An observational distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
over a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
factorizes, according to the chain rule, as

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{v})=\prod_{V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}}p(v_{i}\mid v_{1},\dots,v_{i-1})

However, if we know
P(\mathbf{v})
is compatible with a graph
\mathcal{G}
, CIs implied by
\mathcal{G}
can be used to simplify the factorisation above to a
Markov factorisation
. We first define the Markov factorisation for Markovian DAGs.

(a)
A causal DAG
\mathcal{G}
.

X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{2}
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{1}
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
X_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{4}\mid X_{2}
X_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X_{4}\mid X_{1}
decomposition
weak union
contraction
contraction

(b)
CIs encoded in
\mathcal{G}
.

Figure D.2.1:

A causal DAG
\mathcal{G}
and a hyper-graph depicting all the CIs encoded in
\mathcal{G}
. An edge indicates that we can derive the CIs at the arrowheads from the CIs at the tails using the semi-graphoid axioms. The highlighted subsets of CIs in blue, orange, and pink are each sufficient to derive all other CIs in the graph.

Definition D.2.1

(Markov Relative
(
Bareinboim et al. 2022
)
)
.

An observational distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
is said to be Markov relative to a graph
\mathcal{G}
(over a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
) if, for a given ordering
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
consistent with
\mathcal{G}
,
P(\mathbf{v})
factorizes as

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{v})=\prod_{V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}}p(v_{i}\mid pa^{-}_{i})

(32)

where
Pa^{-}_{i}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{i}\}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{V_{i}\}
.

For example, in the simple three-node graph
X\to Y\to Z
, the observational distribution
P(x,y,z)
factorizes as

\displaystyle P(x)P(y\mid x)P(z\mid y,x)=P(x)P(y\mid x)P(z\mid y)

By means of this factorisation, a graph imposes CI constraints on the distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
: in our example,
Z\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}X\mid Y
. This gives an equivalent definition of ‘Markov relative’:
P(\mathbf{v})
is Markov relative to
\mathcal{G}
if, for a given ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
consistent with
\mathcal{G}
, and for each
V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}
,

\displaystyle V_{i}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{V}^{\leq V_{i}}\setminus\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{i}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\mid\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\{V_{i}\}}\right)\mathclose{}\setminus\{V_{i}\}

Notice how this set of CI constraints is identical to C-LMP in the Markovian case (Eq. (
4
)), discussed in Section
3
. Therefore, if
P(\mathbf{v})
is Markov relative to a given
\mathcal{G}
, all the CI constraints encoded in
\mathcal{G}
must hold in
P(\mathbf{v})
.

If
\mathcal{G}
contains bidirected edges, the factorisation in Def.
D.2.1
no longer applies. For instance, a variable
V_{i}
may be connected to a non-descendant
V_{j}
by means of a bidirected edge,
V_{i}
is not independent of
V_{j}
when conditioning on the (observed) parents
Pa^{-}_{i}
. This leads to the more general definition of compatibility for semi-Markovian graphs, given below.

Definition D.2.2

(Semi-Markov Relative
(
Bareinboim et al. 2022
)
)
.

An observational distribution
P(\mathbf{v})
is said to be Semi-Markov relative to a graph
\mathcal{G}
(over a set of variables
\mathbf{V}
) if, for every ordering
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
consistent with
\mathcal{G}
,
P(\mathbf{v})
factorizes as

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{v})=\prod_{V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}}p(v_{i}\mid pa^{+}_{i})

(33)

where
Pa^{+}_{i}=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(V_{i})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq V_{i}}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{V_{i}\}
.

(a)
X
is separated from
C
but not
A
when conditioning on
B
.

(b)
X
is separated from
A
but not
C
when not conditioning on
B
.

(c)
X
is separated from
F,I
but not
D
when conditioning on
H,E
.

Figure D.2.2:
Fig.
2
reproduced for convenience. Three ACs relative to the variable
X
in the (same) causal DAG
\mathcal{G}
. Assume an ordering
A\prec B\prec\dots\prec X\prec J\prec K
.
The ACs relative to
X
(excluding
\{X\}
itself), shown in blue, separate it from the variables shown in green.

◼ Example D.2.1
.

Consider the semi-Markovian graph
\mathcal{G}
in Fig.
1(a)
. There are 12 possible orderings of the 4 nodes; each ordering induces a factorisation of
P(\mathbf{v})
in Def.
D.2.2
. We give four examples.

1.

X_{1}\prec X_{2}\prec X_{3}\prec X_{4}
. This implies
Pa^{+}(\{X_{1}\})=\emptyset,\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{2}\})=\{X_{1}\},\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{3}\})=\{X_{1},X_{2}\},\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{4}\})=\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
.
The resultant semi-Markov factorisation is
p(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=p(x_{1})p(x_{2}\mid x_{1})p(x_{3}\mid x_{1},x_{2})p(x_{4}\mid x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
.
This is equivalent to the factorisation given by the chain rule, and implies no CI constraints.

2.

X_{1}\prec X_{2}\prec X_{4}\prec X_{3}
. This implies
Pa^{+}(\{X_{1}\})=\emptyset,\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{2}\})=\{X_{1}\},\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{3}\})=\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{4}\},\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{4}\})=\emptyset
.
The resultant semi-Markov factorisation is
p(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=p(x_{1})p(x_{2}\mid x_{1})p(x_{4})p(x_{3}\mid x_{1},x_{2},x_{4})
, which implies the CI constraint
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
.

3.

X_{2}\prec X_{4}\prec X_{1}\prec X_{3}
. This implies
Pa^{+}(\{X_{2}\})=\emptyset,\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{4}\})=\emptyset,\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{1}\})=\{X_{2}\},\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{3}\})=\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{4}\}
.
The resultant semi-Markov factorisation is
p(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=p(x_{2})p(x_{4})p(x_{1}\mid x_{2})p(x_{3}\mid x_{1},x_{2},x_{4})
, which implies the CI constraints
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{2}\}
and
X_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{4}\}\mid\{X_{2}\}
.

4.

X_{4}\prec X_{1}\prec X_{2}\prec X_{3}
. This implies
Pa^{+}(\{X_{4}\})=\emptyset,\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{1}\})=\emptyset,\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{2}\})=\{X_{1}\},\ Pa^{+}(\{X_{3}\})=\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{4}\}
.
The resultant semi-Markov factorisation is
p(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=p(x_{4})p(x_{1})p(x_{2}\mid x_{1})p(x_{3}\mid x_{1},x_{2},x_{4})
, which implies the CI constraints
X_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{4}\}
and
X_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{4}\}\mid\{X_{1}\}
.

The CIs induced by the first ordering (namely, none) are clearly insufficient to derive all CIs encoded in
\mathcal{G}
, similar to Ex.
5
. The four orderings above are chosen to be representative. Each of the eight remaining orderings induces exactly the same CIs as one the four orderings.
∎

We define the exact set of CI constraints implied by the semi-Markov factorisation below.

Definition D.2.3

(Semi-Markov Relative CI Constraints)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a causal graph over variables
\mathbf{V}
and
P(\mathbf{v})
a probability distribution over
\mathbf{V}
that is semi-Markov relative to
\mathcal{G}
. Then, the conditional independence constraints encoded in the factorisation of
P(\mathbf{v})
are given by:
For every ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
consistent with
\mathcal{G}
, for every variable
V_{i}\in\mathbf{V}
,

V_{i}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\mathbf{V}^{\leq V_{i}}\setminus(Pa^{+}(\{V_{i}\})\cup\{V_{i}\})\mid Pa^{+}(\{V_{i}\})

where
Pa^{+}(\{V_{i}\})=\mathit{Pa}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{C}(V_{i})_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq V_{i}}}}}\right)\mathclose{}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathbf{V}^{\leq V_{i}}}}\setminus\{V_{i}\}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\leq V_{i}}
depends on the ordering
\mathbf{V}^{\prec}
.

Note that we take a union over all orderings in Def.
D.2.3
.

◼ Example D.2.2
.

Continuing Ex.
D.2.1
. The set of CIs induced by the semi-Markov factorization for
\mathcal{G}
(Fig.
1(a)
) is the union of all CIs listed in Ex.
D.2.1
:
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\},X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{2}\},X_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{4}\}\mid\{X_{2}\},X_{1}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{4}\},X_{2}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{4}\}\mid\{X_{1}\}
.
∎

Ex.
D.2.1
and Ex.
D.2.2
show an important contrast between the Markovian and semi-Markovian cases. In the Markovian case, we can fix an arbitrary ordering: compatibility requires that
P(\mathbf{v})
factorize according to the product in Def.
D.2.1
for any one ordering.
In the semi-Markovian case, we cannot fix an arbitrary ordering; the ordering
X_{1}\prec X_{2}\prec X_{3}\prec X_{4}
in Ex.
D.2.1
provides no CI constraints.
Coincidentally, the ordering
X_{1}\prec X_{2}\prec X_{4}\prec X_{3}
does suffice to derive all CIs encoded in
\mathcal{G}
from
X_{4}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 3.46875pt{}\kern 3.46875pt\hbox{$\textstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 2.48856pt{}\kern 2.48856pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\perp$}}{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}\hss}\kern 1.78203pt{}\kern 1.78203pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$}}}\{X_{1},X_{2}\}
, as seen in Fig.
1(b)
.
However, no method is known for choosing an ordering (or subset of orderings) that a priori guarantees that all CIs encoded in the graph can be derived from the resulting factorisation(s).
